What brings you to the clinic today?\n\nPatient: I'm a dentist, and I've been trying to plan my retirement and hand over my practice to some younger associates. But lately, my mind is constantly racing about the practice and the future, and I just can’t shut the worrying off anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry, and did it start specifically when you began planning your retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been going on pretty steadily for about a year and a quarter now, maybe 15 months. It definitely started right around the time I began seriously planning my gradual retirement and trying to coordinate the practice buy-in with my younger associates.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this constant worry is strictly focused on your retirement and practice transition, or does it also spill over into other areas of your life, like family, health, or minor daily matters?\n\nPatient: The worry is really centered on the practice transition, my retirement finances, and the security of my staff, rather than things like my health. However, it heavily spills over into my family life because I'm so keyed up that I can't be present with my wife, and I've even started turning down social invitations. It just consumes all my mental energy.\n\nDoctor: Alongside this constant worry, have you also been experiencing a persistently down or depressed mood, or a loss of interest in things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I'm depressed, but I've definitely lost my enjoyment of things and stopped doing them. The constant worry and exhaustion have made me cut back on hobbies I love, like hiking and painting, because even setting up my gear feels like too much of an effort. I've also been turning down social invitations because I just feel too keyed up and drained.\n\nDoctor: Along with feeling keyed up and drained, have you noticed other physical symptoms like muscle tension, trouble sleeping, or finding yourself more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been terrible; I lie awake for hours running numbers in my head and then wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning unable to drift back off. I feel completely drained because of it, but I haven't really noticed any muscle tension or increased irritability.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of worthlessness, hopelessness, or excessive guilt during this difficult period?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or worthless. It's more that I feel a heavy weight of responsibility for my employees' security, which leads me to constantly second-guess my decisions and feel highly stressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since these worries began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or my weight since all of this started. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical sensations of anxiety, such as a racing heart, sweating, dry mouth, or lightheadedness, when these worries are at their worst?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get those kinds of physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating, even when the worry is at its worst. For me, the anxiety is almost entirely mental—just my mind constantly racing, along with the exhaustion and the trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Before you began planning your retirement 15 months ago, did you have a general tendency to worry excessively about everyday things, or is this level of anxiety completely new to you?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, but I don't recall ever worrying to this extreme degree in the past. While I've always been a diligent planner, this level of constant, overwhelming anxiety feels completely new to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty concentrating on daily tasks or making decisions since these worries began?"

It just consumes all my mental energy.\n\nDoctor: Alongside this constant worry, have you also been experiencing a persistently down or depressed mood, or a loss of interest in things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I'm depressed, but I've definitely lost my enjoyment of things and stopped doing them. The constant worry and exhaustion have made me cut back on hobbies I love, like hiking and painting, because even setting up my gear feels like too much of an effort. I've also been turning down social invitations because I just feel too keyed up and drained.\n\nDoctor: Along with feeling keyed up and drained, have you noticed other physical symptoms like muscle tension, trouble sleeping, or finding yourself more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been terrible; I lie awake for hours running numbers in my head and then wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning unable to drift back off. I feel completely drained because of it, but I haven't really noticed any muscle tension or increased irritability.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of worthlessness, hopelessness, or excessive guilt during this difficult period?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or worthless. It's more that I feel a heavy weight of responsibility for my employees' security, which leads me to constantly second-guess my decisions and feel highly stressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since these worries began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or my weight since all of this started. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical sensations of anxiety, such as a racing heart, sweating, dry mouth, or lightheadedness, when these worries are at their worst?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get those kinds of physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating, even when the worry is at its worst. For me, the anxiety is almost entirely mental—just my mind constantly racing, along with the exhaustion and the trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Before you began planning your retirement 15 months ago, did you have a general tendency to worry excessively about everyday things, or is this level of anxiety completely new to you?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, but I don't recall ever worrying to this extreme degree in the past. While I've always been a diligent planner, this level of constant, overwhelming anxiety feels completely new to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty concentrating on daily tasks or making decisions since these worries began?\n\nPatient: Yes, concentration has become a major issue for me, especially at work. I frequently lose my train of thought during dental procedures because my mind drifts to \"what-if\" scenarios, which has led to some small charting and scheduling mistakes. Because of this, I also find myself constantly second-guessing every decision I make.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of self-harm, or felt that life is no longer worth living during this difficult time?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of self-harm or felt that life isn't worth living. While I am extremely stressed and exhausted by this whole situation, those kinds of thoughts have never crossed my mind."

I feel completely drained because of it, but I haven't really noticed any muscle tension or increased irritability.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of worthlessness, hopelessness, or excessive guilt during this difficult period?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or worthless. It's more that I feel a heavy weight of responsibility for my employees' security, which leads me to constantly second-guess my decisions and feel highly stressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since these worries began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or my weight since all of this started. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical sensations of anxiety, such as a racing heart, sweating, dry mouth, or lightheadedness, when these worries are at their worst?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get those kinds of physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating, even when the worry is at its worst. It's more that I feel a heavy weight of responsibility for my employees' security, which leads me to constantly second-guess my decisions and feel highly stressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since these worries began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or my weight since all of this started. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical sensations of anxiety, such as a racing heart, sweating, dry mouth, or lightheadedness, when these worries are at their worst?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get those kinds of physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating, even when the worry is at its worst. For me, the anxiety is almost entirely mental—just my mind constantly racing, along with the exhaustion and the trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Before you began planning your retirement 15 months ago, did you have a general tendency to worry excessively about everyday things, or is this level of anxiety completely new to you?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, but I don't recall ever worrying to this extreme degree in the past. While I've always been a diligent planner, this level of constant, overwhelming anxiety feels completely new to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty concentrating on daily tasks or making decisions since these worries began?\n\nPatient: Yes, concentration has become a major issue for me, especially at work. I frequently lose my train of thought during dental procedures because my mind drifts to \"what-if\" scenarios, which has led to some small charting and scheduling mistakes. Because of this, I also find myself constantly second-guessing every decision I make.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of self-harm, or felt that life is no longer worth living during this difficult time?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of self-harm or felt that life isn't worth living. While I am extremely stressed and exhausted by this whole situation, those kinds of thoughts have never crossed my mind.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing enjoyment in your hobbies and feeling constantly drained. Have these feelings of low energy and lack of interest been present nearly every day for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, it's been happening almost every single day. This constant exhaustion and losing interest in my painting and hiking has been going on steadily for over a year now, ever since I started planning my retirement. It's just incredibly frustrating because no matter what I do, I feel completely drained and can't seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Alongside second-guessing your decisions, have you noticed a significant drop in your overall self-confidence or self-esteem since these difficulties began?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about it as a general drop in my self-esteem. But the constant second-guessing of my decisions definitely makes me feel highly hesitant and unsure of myself lately, especially compared to how confident I used to be."
  }
]

Because of this, I also find myself constantly second-guessing every decision I make.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of self-harm, or felt that life is no longer worth living during this difficult time?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of self-harm or felt that life isn't worth living. While I am extremely stressed and exhausted by this whole situation, those kinds of thoughts have never crossed my mind.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing enjoyment in your hobbies and feeling constantly drained. Have these feelings of low energy and lack of interest been present nearly every day for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, it's been happening almost every single day. This constant exhaustion and losing interest in my painting and hiking has been going on steadily for over a year now, ever since I started planning my retirement. It's just incredibly frustrating because no matter what I do, I feel completely drained and can't seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Alongside second-guessing your decisions, have you noticed a significant drop in your overall self-confidence or self-esteem since these difficulties began?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about it as a general drop in my self-esteem. But the constant second-guessing of my decisions definitely makes me feel highly hesitant and unsure of myself lately, especially compared to how confident I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mentioned you don't feel \"depressed,\" do you ever experience periods of feeling unusually sad, down, or empty? Or is your mood mostly neutral but highly anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel sad, down, or empty. Instead, my mood is entirely dominated by constant, high-level anxiety and tension, and I just feel completely overwhelmed by these racing thoughts and worries about the practice and my retirement."

But lately, my mind is constantly racing about the practice and the future, and I just can’t shut the worrying off anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry, and did it start specifically when you began planning your retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been going on pretty steadily for about a year and a quarter now, maybe 15 months. While I am extremely stressed and exhausted by this whole situation, those kinds of thoughts have never crossed my mind.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing enjoyment in your hobbies and feeling constantly drained. Have these feelings of low energy and lack of interest been present nearly every day for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, it's been happening almost every single day. This constant exhaustion and losing interest in my painting and hiking has been going on steadily for over a year now, ever since I started planning my retirement. It's just incredibly frustrating because no matter what I do, I feel completely drained and can't seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Alongside second-guessing your decisions, have you noticed a significant drop in your overall self-confidence or self-esteem since these difficulties began?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about it as a general drop in my self-esteem. But the constant second-guessing of my decisions definitely makes me feel highly hesitant and unsure of myself lately, especially compared to how confident I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mentioned you don't feel \"depressed,\" do you ever experience periods of feeling unusually sad, down, or empty? Or is your mood mostly neutral but highly anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel sad, down, or empty. Instead, my mood is entirely dominated by constant, high-level anxiety and tension, and I just feel completely overwhelmed by these racing thoughts and worries about the practice and my retirement.\n\nDoctor: When you are completely away from the practice, such as on a weekend or vacation, do these feelings of anxiety and exhaustion temporarily lift, or do they persist regardless of where you are?\n\nPatient: They persist regardless of where I am, and I just can't seem to escape them. Even on vacation, my mind keeps racing about the practice and I can't turn it off, and on my days off I feel so completely drained of energy—like someone pulled the plug on my batteries—that I can't recharge at all."

But lately, my mind is constantly racing about the practice and the future, and I just can’t shut the worrying off anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry, and did it start specifically when you began planning your retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been going on pretty steadily for about a year and a quarter now, maybe 15 months. It definitely started right around the time I began seriously planning my gradual retirement and trying to coordinate the practice buy-in with my younger associates.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this constant worry is strictly focused on your retirement and practice transition, or does it also spill over into other areas of your life, like family, health, or minor daily matters?\n\nPatient: The worry is really centered on the practice transition, my retirement finances, and the security of my staff, rather than things like my health. However, it heavily spills over into my family life because I'm so keyed up that I can't be present with my wife, and I've even started turning down social invitations. It just consumes all my mental energy.\n\nDoctor: Alongside this constant worry, have you also been experiencing a persistently down or depressed mood, or a loss of interest in things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I'm depressed, but I've definitely lost my enjoyment of things and stopped doing them. I feel completely drained because of it, but I haven't really noticed any muscle tension or increased irritability.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of worthlessness, hopelessness, or excessive guilt during this difficult period?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or worthless. It's more that I feel a heavy weight of responsibility for my employees' security, which leads me to constantly second-guess my decisions and feel highly stressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since these worries began?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or my weight since all of this started. My eating habits have stayed pretty much the same despite the stress.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical sensations of anxiety, such as a racing heart, sweating, dry mouth, or lightheadedness, when these worries are at their worst?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get those kinds of physical symptoms like a racing heart or sweating, even when the worry is at its worst. For me, the anxiety is almost entirely mental—just my mind constantly racing, along with the exhaustion and the trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Before you began planning your retirement 15 months ago, did you have a general tendency to worry excessively about everyday things, or is this level of anxiety completely new to you?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, but I don't recall ever worrying to this extreme degree in the past. While I've always been a diligent planner, this level of constant, overwhelming anxiety feels completely new to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty concentrating on daily tasks or making decisions since these worries began?\n\nPatient: Yes, concentration has become a major issue for me, especially at work. I frequently lose my train of thought during dental procedures because my mind drifts to \"what-if\" scenarios, which has led to some small charting and scheduling mistakes. Because of this, I also find myself constantly second-guessing every decision I make.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of self-harm, or felt that life is no longer worth living during this difficult time?\n\nPatient: No, I have never had any thoughts of self-harm or felt that life isn't worth living. While I am extremely stressed and exhausted by this whole situation, those kinds of thoughts have never crossed my mind.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned losing enjoyment in your hobbies and feeling constantly drained. Have these feelings of low energy and lack of interest been present nearly every day for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, it's been happening almost every single day. This constant exhaustion and losing interest in my painting and hiking has been going on steadily for over a year now, ever since I started planning my retirement. It's just incredibly frustrating because no matter what I do, I feel completely drained and can't seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Alongside second-guessing your decisions, have you noticed a significant drop in your overall self-confidence or self-esteem since these difficulties began?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about it as a general drop in my self-esteem. But the constant second-guessing of my decisions definitely makes me feel highly hesitant and unsure of myself lately, especially compared to how confident I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Even though you mentioned you don't feel \"depressed,\" do you ever experience periods of feeling unusually sad, down, or empty? Or is your mood mostly neutral but highly anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel sad, down, or empty. Instead, my mood is entirely dominated by constant, high-level anxiety and tension, and I just feel completely overwhelmed by these racing thoughts and worries about the practice and my retirement.\n\nDoctor: When you are completely away from the practice, such as on a weekend or vacation, do these feelings of anxiety and exhaustion temporarily lift, or do they persist regardless of where you are?\n\nPatient: They persist regardless of where I am, and I just can't seem to escape them. Even on vacation, my mind keeps racing about the practice and I can't turn it off, and on my days off I feel so completely drained of energy—like someone pulled the plug on my batteries—that I can't recharge at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your retirement and the future, do you find yourself holding a very bleak and pessimistic outlook, or do you still feel hopeful that things will turn out well once this transition is over?\n\nPatient: It is very difficult to feel hopeful because my mind constantly defaults to worst-case scenarios and endless \"what-ifs\" about the transition. Instead of looking forward to retirement, I am consumed by worries about whether we'll have enough saved if the corporate chain keeps poaching patients, whether the associates will actually buy in, and what will happen to my staff. All of this constant second-guessing makes the future feel incredibly uncertain and stressful rather than hopeful."
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.
